In our view, a categorical right to counsel in proceedings of the kind before us would carry with it disadvantages (in the form of unfairness and delay) that, in terms of ultimate fairness, would deprive it of significant superiority over the alternatives that we have mentioned. We consequently hold that the Due Process Clause does not *automatically* require the provision of counsel at civil contempt proceedings to an indigent individual who is subject to a child support order, even if that individual faces incarceration (for up to a year). In particular, that Clause does not require the provision of counsel where the opposing parent or other custodian (to whom support funds are owed) is not represented by counsel and the State provides alternative procedural safeguards equivalent to those we have mentioned (adequate notice of the importance of ability to pay, fair opportunity to present, and to dispute, relevant information, and court findings).
We do not address civil contempt proceedings where the underlying child support payment is owed to the State, for example, for reimbursement of welfare funds paid to the parent with custody. [ … ]. Those proceedings more closely resemble debt-collection proceedings. The government is likely to have counsel or some other competent representative. [ … ]And this kind of proceeding is not before us. Neither do we address what due process requires in an unusually complex case where a defendant “can fairly be represented only by a trained advocate.” [ … ]
IV
The record indicates that Turner received neither counsel nor the benefit of alternative procedures like those we have described. He did not receive clear notice that his ability to pay would constitute the critical question in his civil contempt proceeding. No one provided him with a form (or the equivalent) designed to elicit information about his financial circumstances. The court did not find that Turner was able to pay his arrearage, but instead left the relevant “finding” section of the contempt order blank. The court nonetheless found Turner in contempt and ordered him incarcerated. Under these circumstances Turner’s incarceration violated the Due Process Clause.
We vacate the judgment of the South Carolina Supreme Court and remand the case for further proceedings not inconsistent with this opinion.
*It is so ordered.*
Justice Thomas,
with whom Justice Scalia joins, and with whom The Chief Justice and Justice Alito join as to Parts I-B and II,
dissenting.***
The Due Process Clause of the Fourteenth Amendment does not provide a right to appointed counsel for indigent defendants facing incarceration in civil contempt proceedings. Therefore, I would affirm. Although the Court agrees that appointed counsel was not required in this case, it nevertheless vacates the judgment of the South Carolina Supreme Court on a different ground, which the parties have never raised. Solely at the invitation of the United States as *amicus curiae,* the majority decides that Turner's contempt proceeding violated due process because it did not include “alternative procedural safeguards.” [ … ] Consistent with this Court’s longstanding practice, I would not reach that question.
[ … ]

24.4

### Triaging Appointed-Counsel Funding


Read the sections of "Triaging Appointed-Counsel Funding and Pro Se Access to Justice" by Barton and Bibas found  (pdf).

Citation: Benjamin H. Barton & Stephanos Bibas, "Triaging Appointed-Counsel Funding and Pro Se Access to Justice," 160 **U. Pa. L. Rev.** 967 (2012).

24.5

### It's Not Triage if the Patient Bleeds Out

# SOURCE: content/course/casebook/CasebookIntrosClasses01To08Version20260323.md — Casebook Introductions: Classes 1–8

<!-- page: 1 -->

## Day 1: Overview, SMJ p.1 — AUJ

The United States has both federal and state judicial systems. For either a federal or state court to hear a case, it must have *jurisdiction*. Actually, there are several kinds of jurisdiction. In this course, you will learn about two of them, subject matter jurisdiction and personal jurisdiction. The first four class periods deal with subject matter jurisdiction, which governs the type of case that a court can hear.

Let's start with subject matter jurisdiction in the state courts. For the purposes of this class, state courts are courts of *general jurisdiction*, meaning that as far as subject matter jurisdiction goes, they have the power to hear any type of case. In other words, the state courts are ready and willing to hear cases about anything and everything. Sometimes, the federal government (specifically, Congress) says that the state courts cannot hear a case involving a certain subject matter. When that happens, we say that there is *exclusive federal jurisdiction*, meaning only the federal courts can hear that type of case. That's a federal government decision, though, it's not the state courts refusing to hear something. And by the way, exclusive federal jurisdiction is rare. For your purposes, it applies only to lawsuits involving federal antitrust statutes, intellectual property (copyright, trademark, patent), and the United States as a party.

Federal court are different. For a federal court to hear a case, it must have *subject matter jurisdiction* (SMJ). If a federal court lacks SMJ, the plaintiff must file in state court. What happens if both a state and federal court have jurisdiction (*concurrent jurisdiction*)? The plaintiff has a choice! In that case, which court should they file in? What factors should they consider in deciding where to file? What consequences could arise from this decision?

For the next few days, we will be discussing how a federal court can obtain SMJ. One way is through *arising under jurisdiction*, which means that the case *arises under* federal law (although . . . it's complicated). Today we will consider: How does a federal court obtain arising under jurisdiction? Where does the federal court's power to hear cases "arising under" federal law come from? How broad is the "arising under" power? Can a party get a case into federal court under the "arising under" power through something called a declaratory judgement action?

---

## Day 2: SMJ p.2 — Diversity Jurisdiction, begin SuppJ

Yesterday, you learned that a federal court can obtain SMJ through arising under jurisdiction, or when a case *arises under* federal law. Today, you will learn a second way a federal court can obtain SMJ: diversity jurisdiction. Diversity jurisdiction refers to cases "between Citizens of different States." We will consider: What does it mean to be "Citizens of different States"? Where does the federal court's power to hear cases "between Citizens of different States" come from? What are the justifications for and criticisms of diversity jurisdiction? Should the scope of diversity jurisdiction be curtailed or expanded?

For a federal court to have diversity jurisdiction, two equally important requirements must be met. If either of these requirements are not met, there can be no diversity jurisdiction.

<!-- page: 2 -->

First, the parties to the case must have *complete diversity*. Complete diversity means **all** plaintiffs are diverse from all defendants. It does not matter if multiple parties on the same side of the *v.* have the same citizenship, so long as all parties on the left of the *v.* are diverse from all parties on the right of the v. You will also learn the rules of citizenship: when a human being, a corporation, or an unincorporated association (a labor union, a charity, a partnership) is a citizens of a particular state.

Second, the amount in controversy must exceed $75,000. For the purposes of our class, this can only be met if one plaintiff sues one defendant with one claim exceeding $75k, OR if one plaintiff sues the same defendant with multiple claims that add up to exceed $75k. The latter circumstances is called "stacking."

We may also begin our discussion of the third way a federal court can obtain SMJ: supplemental jurisdiction.

---

## Day 3: SMJ p.3 — SuppJ and Removal Jurisdiction

So far, you have learned two ways a federal court can obtain SMJ: arising under jurisdiction and diversity jurisdiction. Today we will discuss the third way a federal court can hear a claim: supplemental jurisdiction. Supplemental jurisdiction applies when there are multiple claims in a single federal court lawsuit. For at least one of those claims, either arising under or diversity provides a bases of SMJ. We say that for those claims for which arising under or diversity is available, there is "original" SMJ or an "independent" basis of SMJ. In class, we will refer to this claim (or these claims) as the "anchoring claim(s)." But for other claims, there is neither arising under or diversity. So for these claims, there is no original SMJ or independent basis of SMJ. Note: If federal law creates a claim, there is arising under definition under 1331. For that reason, we often say that supplemental SMJ might be necessary for a "state law claim." Don't get confused. All diversity claims are state law claims. So the "state law claim" that might need supplemental SMJ is one that either (i) lacks complete diversity, or (ii) is AI deficient.

Federal courts have discretion to deny supplemental jurisdiction. That's unusual!

We will also consider: Are there different considerations if the anchoring claim created SMJ under arising under jurisdiction or diversity jurisdiction? What factors does a federal court consider when exercising their discretion to deny supplemental jurisdiction?

We may also begin removal jurisdiction, which allows a party (typically the defendant) to transfer a case from a state court to federal court. What are the justifications for removal jurisdiction? Why would a defendant want to remove their case to federal court? What are the rules for removal? When will a case be remanded (sent back to state court)?

---

## Day 4: SMJ p.4 — Abstention

So far, we have discussed the ways a federal court can obtain SMJ over a claim: arising under jurisdiction, diversity jurisdiction, and supplemental jurisdiction. Are there any occasions on which a federal court will refuse to hear a case, despite having SMJ? This is known as abstention. Abstention is pretty rare and only occurs for a few reasons.

<!-- page: 3 -->

First, even if the requirements for diversity jurisdiction are met, a federal court will not hear marital (divorce) or probate cases. These are called the martial and probate exceptions to diversity jurisdiction.

Second, a federal court might abstain from hearing a case if there is simultaneous ongoing federal and state court litigation among substantially the same parties on substantially the same issues. Why would a federal court wish to abstain in this case? How often does the court do this?

---

## Day 5: HCOL (completed by BEN & SUNNY)

Sometimes, the transaction giving rise to a lawsuit occurs across state boundaries. Perhaps a party in State A makes a contract in State B, and the party in State A later alleges that the other party breached. Perhaps a set of events giving rise to a tort begins in State A and finishes in State B. If the laws of State A and State B are the same, there is no problem about how to decide a case. But, if the law of State A differs from the law of State B, which governs the case? That is the subject for today.

---

## Day 6: IPJ p.2

Up until this point, we have spent our time discussing federal court subject matter jurisdiction, which is all about what type of case a federal court can hear. Today, we will be moving on to personal jurisdiction. Personal jurisdiction answers a different question: what are the geographic limits on a court's power? In other words, where, geographically, can you file a lawsuit?

Consider: if a plaintiff from South Carolina was in a car accident in Nebraska with a Defendant from North Carolina, we would all logically agree she cannot sue in Hawaii. But why, if she has SMJ in federal court through diversity jurisdiction? The answer is that no Hawaii court, state or federal, would have personal jurisdiction over the defendant.

For the purposes of our class, federal courts and state courts are the same when it comes to personal jurisdiction. That's a big difference from SMJ. Remember that class periods 1–4 concerned SMJ in the federal courts; state courts were willing to hear cases about anything, and Congress only rarely lodged exclusive SMJ in the federal courts.

You will learn about three types of personal jurisdiction: in personam, in rem, and quasi in rem. Let's start with in personam jurisdiction (IPJ). In personam personal jurisdiction is a basis for jurisdiction over a person. How do you get it? There are two primary ways: general and specific. Today, you will learn the answers to the following: What are the ways to obtain general IPJ? What is the specific IPJ test? What options does a defendant have when a plaintiff files a lawsuit?

---

## Day 7: IPJ p.3

Yesterday, you learned several bases for general in personam jurisdiction (the defendant is really at home; the defendant is *Daimler* "at home;" the defendant consents via failure to

<!-- page: 4 -->

object or a forum selection clause; or (maybe?) the defendant is physically tagged.) You also learned that specific in-personam jurisdiction requires a long arm statute; minimal contacts linking the defendant, subject matter of litigation, and forum; and the *McGee* fair and reasonable factors.

When determining whether there are minimal contacts for specific IPJ, you might look towards whether the defendant purposefully availed themselves of the privileges of the forum state. This is relatively easy to determine when dealing with intentional torts or contracts cases — for example, if you sell cars in Georgia, you have likely availed yourself of the privilege of doing business in Georgia.

But what about in the case of unintentional torts? And what about outside the commercial context, such as cases involve family law and child support payments? These cases are harder to determine.

We will also move on to our discussion on quasi-in rem and in-rem jurisdiction today. In-rem and quasi-in-rem are asserting personal jurisdiction over a piece of property. This piece of property must be in the forum state. We will run into the same problem we saw on Day 2, when we discussed locating incorporeal entities for purposes of diversity jurisdiction. For example, how can we locate personal debt? Bonds? Stocks? Also, does the exercise of in-rem or quasi-in rem jurisdiction require an analysis of minimum contacts, as required for in-personam jurisdiction?

---

## Day 8: IPJ p.4

As we conclude our study of jurisdiction over person and property, we will focus on three unclear areas of law related to personal jurisdiction.

1. As you learned yesterday, there are two ways to think about quasi-in-rem jurisdiction following *Schaffer*. One option is that quasi-in-rem jurisdiction lives on. The second option is that a court cannot exercise quasi-in-rem jurisdiction unless the property had a relationship to the subject matter of the dispute. This view practically eliminates the entire point of quasi-in-rem jurisdiction. Which is correct?

2. If we adopt the view of quasi-in-rem jurisdiction that it requires a minimum contacts analysis, we must consider its impacts on in-personam jurisdiction. Are all traditional bases for exercising IPJ unconstitutional if they do not provide minimal contacts? Is an in-state tag sufficient for a court to exercise personal jurisdiction? Consider the extreme case of someone who is tagged in an airplane flying over a state.

3. How do we consider in-personal jurisdiction as it relates to online events?

# SOURCE: content/course/casebook/CasebookIntrosClasses18To24Version20260319.md — Casebook Introductions: Classes 18–24

<!-- page: 1 -->

## Day 18: Appellate Litigation

A trial court judgment is not necessarily the end of parties' legal conflict. Parties can appeal any decision a trial judge makes to a higher court. A key question: when in a proceeding may a party who does not like a trial court order appeal it? Should a party be able to appeal any trial court order immediately after the trial court issues it? Or should the party have to wait, and if so, until when?

We will study only the federal system's answers to these questions. State systems have different answers, but you are not responsible for them in this class.

In the federal system, the doctrine that determines when a party may appeal a trial court decision is called "appellate jurisdiction." Like a trial court, a court of appeals must have jurisdiction over a proceeding to hear it. For the purposes of our class, the source of the appellate court's jurisdiction turns on the nature of the decision below. In most instances, we need to know whether the lower court decision was a "final order." The court system is busy, and we assume that the trial court usually decides things correctly, so we only allow for appeals at the end. Today, we will discuss what constitutes a final order, as well as a few key exceptions to the rule that a litigant must wait until a trial court issues a final order to appeal any decision that led up to the final order. These exceptions allow litigants to seek appellate review in the middle of a case (prior to the trial court's final judgment). We call such appeals "interlocutory."

---

## Day 19: Expanding the Lawsuit

Lawsuits are long and expensive. It is more efficient for the court system when parties sue everyone related to a given controversy at once on as many possible theories of relief as once. Until now, we've focused almost entirely on suits between one plaintiff and one defendant. Today, we will be expanding the lawsuit. One limit on who parties can add to a suit comes from what we've learned earlier in class: courts must have subject matter jurisdiction and personal jurisdiction, and venue must be proper over each of the claims they hear. We will also discuss rules for who can — and sometimes, who must — be part of the expanded lawsuit, and what sorts of claims parties can add against one another. Those rules are literally rules: the Federal Rules of Civil Procedure.

To repeat: for each claim and each party a litigant wants to add to the basic structure of single plaintiff sues single defendant on a single cause of action, the addition of the claim or party must be copacetic under (i) the FRCP, (ii) SMJ doctrine, and (iii) IPJ doctrine. In addition, venue must be proper with respect to the first set of claims that plaintiff(s) file(s) against defendant(s).

---

## Day 20: Claim Preclusion

As we learned last class, the court system wants to encourage efficiency by allowing, sometimes requiring, the addition of parties and claims to a lawsuit. One way courts enforce this desire for efficiency is through claim preclusion. Under claim preclusion, a party is barred from bringing a future suit on a "claim" that has already been litigated. Beware: the word "claim" in this context has a meaning totally and completely different from what it meant in the previous class period.

For claim preclusion to apply, three key elements must be present: (1) same claim, (2) same party, (3) a judgement that is final and on the merits. Today, we will discuss what each of these elements actually entails, as well as a few stealth factors that impact whether a party is able to bring their claim.

<!-- page: 2 -->

## Day 21: Issue Preclusion

Last class we learned about claim preclusion. Issue preclusion is a totally different doctrine, and it is critically important to keep the two separated in your head, your notes, and (eventually) your outline.

Under issue preclusion (sometimes called collateral estoppel), a party can bar another party from relitigating an issue that was litigated previously; for issue preclusion to work, the party to be barred had to have lost on that issue in the previous litigation. An issue is much narrower than a claim and is usually factual. Issue preclusion requires four elements to run: (1) same issue, (2) actually litigated, (3) necessary to the decision, (4) mutuality. Today, we will discuss each of these elements, as well as a few stealth factors that impact whether a party is able to invoke issue preclusion.

---

## Day 22: Intersystem Preclusion, Agency Preclusion, Class Actions 1

Until today, we have discussed examples where Lawsuit 1 and Lawsuit 2 happen in the same court system. But what happens for claim and issue preclusion when they happen in different court systems? We have learned several instances where preclusion law may differ by jurisdiction.

Article IV Section 1 of the Constitution tells us that states must give full faith and credit to judicial decisions in other states but is silent as to what happens when L1 or L2 happens in federal court. 28 USC § 1738 extends full faith and credit to situations where L1 occurs in state court and L2 is brought in federal court, and federal common law dictates the rest. Today, we will learn how to apply these different sources of law to determine whose preclusion law to apply when Lawsuit 1 and Lawsuit 2 occur in different jurisdictions. And please remember, this may sound like an *Erie* or HCOL issue, but it is not! Don't be the person who confuses them.

Lastly, what happens if the rendering adjudicator is an agency? When must a subsequent adjudicator (like a state or federal court, or another agency) give preclusive effect to the agency decision? For an agency decision to have preclusive effect, the agency must provide due process protections discussed earlier in the course.

We will also begin our discussion of class action lawsuits today. As we've learned throughout class, lawsuits are extremely costly and our legal system is incredibly complex, making it inaccessible to most people. When small wrongs have happened to lots of people, they face a collective action problem. Class actions provide one solution. Is a class action lawsuit a procedural aggregation device or the creation of a new, single legal entity? We're not really sure. Today, we will discuss these two different treatments as it results to SMJ, IPJ, and venue for class action lawsuits. We will also discuss the prerequisites to forming a class: numerosity, commonality, typicality, and representativeness, as well as a few other requirements that may attach depending on the type of class action you are filing.

---

## Day 23: Class Actions 2, Complex Cases 1

Over the course of the semester, we've learned about many civil procedure topics in isolation. Today, we will begin the discussion of two cases that combine many of these concepts.

---

## Day 24: Complex Cases 2, A2J

<!-- page: 3 -->

It wouldn't be the last day of Civil Procedure without *Erie*. Today, we will be discussing another complex case that integrates class actions and VCOL and also serves as the Supreme Court's best articulation of the different *Erie* tests. Pay particular attention to the Scalia and Ginsburg opinions.

But how does everything we've learned this semester play out for normal people trying to access the legal system? Our final readings investigate some of these access to justice issues and should get you thinking about what types of reforms we should make.

# SOURCE: content/course/casebook/Ford Clean Final.md — Ford Motor Co. v. Montana Eighth Judicial District Court (2021)

<!-- page: 1 -->

## *Ford Motor Co. v. Montana Eighth Judicial District Court* (2021)

In *Ford*, the United States Supreme Court established that a state may exercise specific personal jurisdiction over a company that (i) sold products of the type that allegedly caused the plaintiff's injury in the forum state (as it did in all 50 states), and (ii) took steps, successful and profitable steps, to create a market for used versions of those products, but (iii) did not sell the particular allegedly defective product that caused the plaintiff's injury to the plaintiff, nor (iv) sell that particular product to the consumer who did buy it in the forum. Instead, the plaintiff bought the product in used form from a prior consumer, who had purchased it (from Ford) in a different state. The Court held that because Ford had systematically served the market (primary and used) in Montana and Minnesota (the two fora at issue) for the vehicle types that the plaintiffs alleged had malfunctioned in those States, there were sufficiently strong contacts among the defendant, the forum, and the subject matter of litigation for the state to exercise specific personal jurisdiction.

Two plaintiffs — one the estate of a Montana resident (Ms. Gullett) and one a Minnesota resident (Mr. Bandemer) — brought separate tort claims (products liability, failure to warn, and negligence) in their respective states against Ford. Ms. Gullett's suit alleged that a malfunction in her Ford Explorer resulted in a fatal accident in Montana. Mr. Bandemer's suit alleged that a Crown Victoria's defective airbags caused him serious injury during a collision in Minnesota. Ford designed both vehicles in Michigan and manufactured them in Kentucky or Canada. Ford originally sold the cars at issue outside the forum states — the Explorer in Washington, the Crown Victoria in North Dakota. Later resales and relocations in the used car market brought the vehicles to Montana and Minnesota.

Ford moved to dismiss both suits for lack of personal jurisdiction. Ford admitted it had "purposefully availed itself of the privilege of conducting activities" in both states but claimed that, for the plaintiffs to exercise specific personal jurisdiction, a causal link occurring in or relating to the forum was required between Ford and the incident at issue. Ford asserted that such a link only existed "if the company had designed, manufactured, or sold in the State the particular vehicle involved in the accident." *Ford Motor*, 592 U.S. 351, 356–357. Both states' supreme courts rejected Ford's argument. The Montana court pointed to the 36 Ford dealerships within the state. The Minnesota court pointed to 2,000 Crown Victorias that had been sold in Minnesota and Ford's 84 dealerships as evidence of Ford's targeted marketing.

Ford appealed these holdings to the United States Supreme Court.

The Supreme Court affirmed both decisions, finding that "[w]hen a company like Ford serves a market for a product in a State and that product causes injury in the State to one of its residents, the State's courts may entertain the resulting suit." 592 U.S. at 355. The Court held that the phrases "arise out of" and "relate to" from its previous SIPJ cases were alternative pathways to establish SIPJ. A claim could therefore relate to the defendant's contacts with the

<!-- page: 2 -->

forum, even absent causation, if a defendant "serve[d] a market for a product in the forum State and the product malfunctions there." *Id.* at 352.

Ford served "the market" in Montana and Minnesota by encouraging people to drive their cars into those states. Ford, by advertising to Montanans and Minnesotans to persuade them buy its vehicles (including its used vehicles), served the market and thus purposefully availed themselves of the right to do business in the states, despite there (allegedly) being no direct causal relationship among Ford, the forum states, and the specific accidents at hand. Further, the court found that it would make less jurisdictional sense to channel the suits to the states of first sale — Washington and South Dakota — as neither of the accidents occurred there and neither of the plaintiffs were citizens of those states.

**Questions:**

1. What distinguishes this case from the stream of commerce theory that the *Nicastro* plurality tried so hard to discredit? Ford sells to a consumer in State A, who then resells to another consumer in State B, who suffers an injury and sues in the state in which the injury occurred. SCOTUS holds that there is IPJ. McIntyre UK sells to McIntyre USA in country A, who then resells to a consuming company state B, whose employee suffers an injury and sues in the state in which the injury occurred. Both are products liability cases (meaning the same causes of action are asserted). SCOTUS holds that there is no IPJ. What distinguishes the two cases?

2. Similarly, which facts distinguish *Ford* from *World-Wide Volkswagen* (which, you'll remember, held that the Oklahoma court could not exercise personal jurisdiction over a northeastern automobile retailer and its wholesale distributor with respect to a car that the consumer drove to the forum state)?

3. In his concurrence in *Ford*, Justice Gorsuch suggests any state court should be able to exercise personal jurisdiction over any nationwide corporation regardless of any "case-links." The majority rejects this assertion, claiming that under Gorsuch's view a California court could hear a claim against Ford brought by an Ohio plaintiff based on an accident occurring in Ohio involving a car purchased in Ohio. If personal jurisdiction is about satisfying due process through (usually fictional) "consent," does this hypothetical case violate Ford's rights? What if personal jurisdiction is about "traditional notions of fair play and substantial justice?" What about Ford's ability to reasonably anticipate being hauled into court in California? (Surely there is no argument that a company like Ford is non-trivially inconvenienced by the prospect of defending a lawsuit anywhere in the United States?)

---

## Lower Court Split: Different Markets Theory

In the past few years global manufacturers have argued an exception to *Ford*. They claim that, even where all the *Ford* conditions are met, their contacts with the forum state are not

<!-- page: 3 -->

'related' to the suit (and thus the state may not exercise SIPJ) if the company took steps to limit its sales to particular buyers within the state's market. This "different-markets theory" involves splitting the forum state's market for a product into submarkets, then identifying which submarket is the right one in which to conduct the personal jurisdiction inquiry. *See Ethridge v. Samsung SDI Co., Ltd.*, 137 F.4th 309, 318 (5th Cir.), *opinion withdrawn and superseded on reh'g*, 163 F.4th 136 (5th Cir. 2025).

For example, in *Yamashita v. LG Chem*, the Plaintiff brought a products-liability suit in Hawaii against LGC claiming that it negligently manufactured and distributed a 18650 lithium-ion battery that the Plaintiff used to power an electronic cigarette that subsequently exploded in his mouth. 62 F.4th 496, 508 (9th Cir. 2023). Plaintiff had bought the 18650 battery through an unidentified third-party reseller. LGC employees denied that the firm authorized any third-party retailer or distributor to sell stand-alone batteries in Hawaii. Nonetheless, the Plaintiff claimed the accident sufficiently related to LGC's conduct in Hawaii because LGC had shipped lithium-ion batteries and the raw materials to build 18650 batteries to and through Hawaii. LGC alleged, however, that these shipments did not contain any 18650 batteries. Further, LGC argued it sought to limit its sales in Hawaii to residential solar batteries, which were large batteries installed in solar-power systems and were different than the small, portable stand-alone 18650 battery involved in the Plaintiff's accident. LGC argued there was no relatedness between their forum contact (through shipments and solar batteries) and the Plaintiff's injury.

The 9th Circuit held that because the LGC had sought to limits its sales to a submarket other than the one that had resulted in the plaintiff's purchase, LGC contacts did not sufficiently "relate to" the plaintiff's injuries to exert SIPJ over LGC. The 9th Circuit held that "*Ford* gives little reason to think that the relatedness prong would have been satisfied if, for example, Ford had sold Crown Victorias only to police departments in Minnesota, had not marketed them to consumers, and had not serviced them at all. Such contacts would not typically cause, and could not be foreseen to cause, injuries resulting from consumer ownership of Crown Victorias, especially if most consumer-owned Crown Victorias were acquired out-of-state. Similarly, even if LGC sold 18650 batteries to manufacturers for incorporation in consumer products sold in Hawaii, these sales would not be related to purchases of stand-alone batteries by Hawaii consumers." *Yamashita*, 62 F.4th at 508.

In a similar, battery-related case, the Seventh Circuit found an Indiana district court could not exercise SIPJ over a manufacturer who sold to third parties that then sold to customers in Indiana. The court pointed to evidence that the company had taken steps to ensure its customers used the batteries only for approved purposes and tried to prevent sales to individual consumers like the plaintiff. *B.D. by & through Myers v. Samsung Sdi Co.*, 143 F.4th 757 (7th Cir. 2025).

To date, the Sixth Circuit, the Texas Supreme Court, the Mississippi Supreme Court, and an intermediate appellate court in Minnesota have rejected the different-markets theory. *See Sullivan v. LG Chem, Ltd.*, 79 F.4th 651 (6th Cir. 2023) (holding that a Michigan district court could exercise personal jurisdiction over a company in a suit by a plaintiff whose vape exploded because the company directly shipped batteries into Michigan even though the company never

<!-- page: 4 -->

directly sold to vape stores or individual consumers in Michigan); *LG Chem Am., Inc. v. Morgan*, 670 S.W.3d 341 (Tex. 2023) (holding that a consumer's claim was sufficiently related to manufacturer's and distributor's contacts with Texas to satisfy due process for exercise of personal jurisdiction); *Dilworth v. LG Chem, Ltd.*, 355 So. 3d 201 (Miss. 2022) (holding the battery manufacturer purposefully availed itself of the market for lithium-ion batteries in Mississippi, such that the exercise of personal jurisdiction was fundamentally reasonable and fair); *Peters v. Samsung SDI Co.*, No. A25-0195, 2025 WL 2902144 (Minn. Ct. App. Oct. 13, 2025), *review denied* (Dec. 31, 2025); *LG Chem, Ltd. v. Lemmerman*, 863 S.E.2d 514 (2021).

The Fifth, Seventh, and Ninth Circuits, the Nevada Supreme Court, and an intermediate appellate court in California have accepted the different markets theory. *See Ethridge v. Samsung SDI Co., Ltd.*, 163 F.4th 136 (5th Cir. 2025); *Franceschi v. LG Chem, Ltd.*, 580 P.3d 1279 (2025) (upholding the lower court's dismissal of the lawsuit for lack of personal jurisdiction because, while the company purposefully availed itself the Nevada market through certain contacts, the harm alleged by plaintiff did not arise out of or relate to those contacts); *Chem, Ltd. v. Superior Ct.*, 295 Cal. Rptr. 3d 661 (2022) (holding that personal jurisdiction could not be exerted over the battery company because the plaintiff's personal injury claims did not arise out of or relate to the company's sale of car batteries to California).

The Fifth Circuit landed on the opposite side of the split from the Texas and Mississippi state Supreme Courts within it, creating a particularly untenable problem: a suit might proceed in state court with SIPJ, but if it were removed to the federal court, where Fifth Circuit precedent governs, it would be dismissed for lack of personal jurisdiction. That should trouble you, as FRCP 4(k)(1)(A) generally seeks to ensure coherence in the personal jurisdiction analyses of federal courts and the state courts in which they sit. Expect more on the extent to which we want uniformity between state and federal courts when we discuss the *Erie* doctrine. It is likely that the Supreme Court will soon grant certiorari in a different markets case to resolve the emerging within-circuit conflicts.

**Questions:**

1. Putting all of the analysis you just read aside, if you had tried to apply the rule we've learned from *International Shoe* which requires (i) a long arm statute, (ii) minimal contacts linking defendant, subject matter of litigation, forum, and (iii) fair and reasonable considerations, would you have ruled the forum could exercise SIPJ over *Ford*? What about *Yamashita*?

2. Do you find the "different-markets theory" persuasive? As a judge, what evidence would you demand when determining if a corporation targeted one market within a forum but not another?

3. The 9th Circuit suggests the "relatedness" prong for SIPJ would not have been met if Ford had sold Crown Victorias only to police departments in Minnesota, had not marketed them to consumers, and had not serviced them. Do you agree? Do you think all

<!-- page: 5 -->

*three* prongs should be necessary conditions for Minnesota to exercise SIPJ over Ford? Is one prong more or less critical to this analysis than the others?

4. Let's say one of these potential different markets cases is filed in a Texas state court and the defendant removes to the United States District Court for the Eastern District of Texas. Think back to when we learned removal (28 U.S.C. § 1441). Does it say anything about remanding to the lower court over *personal* jurisdiction? Assuming the federal court does not remand, should they apply the precedent of the 5th Circuit (no personal jurisdiction) or Texas law (court has personal jurisdiction)? You will soon learn about what to do when there is a conflict between federal and state laws with *Erie*, but how do you think courts should handle this?

# SOURCE: content/course/casebook/walden-berk-writeups.md — Walden v. Fiore and Berk v. Choy — Case Writeups

<!-- page: 1 -->

## *Walden v. Fiore*

A pair of Nevada residents were traveling home from Puerto Rico. They had a layover in Atlanta. TSA searched their bags at the Puerto Rico airport and found $97,000 in cash. The Nevadans informed TSA that they were professional gamblers, and the money was their starter stake and winnings from the trip. TSA allowed them to board the flight to Atlanta with their cash, but they alerted the DEA. At the Atlanta airport, Walden, a Georgia DEA agent, stopped the Nevadans and seized their money on the grounds that it constituted the proceeds of illegal activity. According to the Nevadans' subsequent lawsuit (you will later learn that because of the procedural posture of the case, the court had to assume that the following facts were true), Walden assisted in drafting a false probable cause affidavit that would enable the government to "forfeit," meaning to seek a court order to allowing the government to become the owner of, the seized funds. Walden sent that affidavit to the US Attorney's Office in Georgia, but it was never acted upon, meaning the U.S. Attorney never filed a forfeiture lawsuit. Plaintiffs' funds were returned eight-to-nine months afterwards. Plaintiffs filed a tort claim against Walden in the District Court of Nevada based on Walden's search, seizure, and false affidavit. The district court found that it lacked personal jurisdiction over Walden in Nevada, but the Ninth Circuit reversed. The Ninth Circuit noted that Walden knew that the affidavit would injure the Nevada plaintiffs in Nevada, and that some of the cash originated from Nevada. According to the Circuit, these facts were enough to establish a basis for personal jurisdiction.

The Supreme Court reversed, concluding that the Nevada District Court for Nevada did not have personal jurisdiction over Walden. The Court held: "[T]he plaintiff cannot be the only link between the defendant and the forum. Rather, it is the defendant's conduct that must form the necessary connection with the forum State." There will be times when the defendant's contacts with the forum are wrapped up with their relationship to the plaintiff, but that relationship, absent those contacts with the forum, is insufficient to establish personal jurisdiction on its own. Applying those principles to the case, Justice Thomas for a unanimous Court concluded that "none of [Walden's] challenged conduct had anything to do with Nevada itself." Nor did the injury suffered by the plaintiffs (the seizure of funds) have anything to do with Nevada itself, because the State was only implicated by the plaintiffs' independent choice to return there. There was thus no personal jurisdiction over Walden in Nevada.

But wait...what about *Calder v. Jones*? Didn't that case allow personal jurisdiction where the defendant knew that their actions would cause injury to a plaintiff in the forum state? Not so, said Justice Thomas. He reasoned that the reputational injury in *Calder* would not have occurred but for the fact that the defendant wrote an article for publication in the forum state that was subsequently read by other citizens of the forum about events that occurred in the forum. In this way, the reputational injury was tied up with the forum itself, not just the plaintiff. Here, however, the plaintiffs would have suffered the same loss of funds no matter where they were, Nevada or otherwise. Is this convincing?

<!-- page: 2 -->

## *Berk v. Choy*

Harold Berk, on a seemingly innocuous trip to Delaware, broke his ankle after falling out of bed. His resulting hospital stay would kickstart the Court's latest chapter in the *Erie* saga. While the details do not matter, the upshot is that Berk sued his physician, Dr. Choy, and the hospital for medical malpractice (a state law cause of action) in federal court, alleging diversity jurisdiction. Under Delaware law, a complaint for medical malpractice must include an affidavit of merit signed by a medical practitioner in which the practitioner affirms under penalty of perjury that there were reasonable grounds to believe that the defendant committed malpractice. (The Federal Rules of Civil Procedure, as you know, contain no such requirement.) Berk did not file the affidavit, and the District Court dismissed the lawsuit. The Third Circuit affirmed, holding that the Delaware law was substantive and therefore applied in federal court.

The Supreme Court reversed, holding that the Delaware law did not apply in federal court. Justice Barrett, writing for eight justices, held that the Delaware law requiring an affidavit at the complaint stage conflicted with FRCPs 8(a) and 12(b). Both provisions addressed what information a plaintiff must provide about their claim at the first step of the litigation. Rule 8, she reasoned, established both the minimum and maximum pleading standards in federal court. Rule 8 requires only a short plain statement showing entitlement to relief. Delaware law therefore conflicted with the Federal Rules because the state provision required both a pleading and evidence of a malpractice claim (the affidavit) for the lawsuit to proceed. Rule 12(b)(6), Justice Barrett further reasoned, listed the grounds for which a federal court complaint could be dismissed for inadequacy, and it made no mention of an affidavit. As interpreted by the Delaware Supreme Court, failure to file an affidavit of merit in a malpractice claim constitutes grounds for dismissal of the case, implying that a federal court must look beyond the four corners of the complaint in ruling on a motion to dismiss. You know from *Twombly*, however, that motions to dismiss under the Federal Rules are evaluated only on the basis of the factual allegations in the complaint itself. Delaware law thus conflicted with the Federal Rules.

Justice Barrett then considered whether Rule 8 "really regulate[d] procedure" under *Sibbach*. Like all the federal rules examined in *Erie* cases, Rule 8 did, in fact, regulate procedure. Despite Rule 8's indirect effects on substantive rights, it regulated process, not "the rights themselves, the available remedies, or rules of decision." The Court also held that Rule 8 applied to the issue at hand because it specified what a plaintiff had to provide at the complaint stage, namely, a short and plain statement showing entitlement to relief and nothing else. The Court ended its opinion with a terse rejection of Justice Stevens's reliance on the Rules Enabling Act in *Shady Grove*, noting in dicta that it was irrelevant whether the displaced state law was procedural.

On its face, the *Berk* opinion is unremarkable. The news in the case is the dog that did not bark, or perhaps, the dog that did not consider barking. At no point did Justice Barrett consider narrowly interpreting the federal rules to accommodate Delaware's interest in protecting its medical services community from certain kinds of malpractice lawsuits. Couldn't the Court, had it so desired,

<!-- page: 3 -->

have held that Rule 8 specified that a federal court complaint must include a short plain statement showing entitlement to relief but was silent on whether anything more was required? Given the long-running drumbeat from the medical community that defensive medicine drives up health care costs, couldn't Delaware (and the many states like it that have similar laws) have pointed to powerful concerns about primary conduct that supported its law? Why didn't Justice Barrett at least consider reading Rules 8 and 12 "with sensitivity to important state interests," as five justices had insisted be done in *Shady Grove*?
